The framework of the auxiliary group: two birds with one stone
R. Krishnan

Abstract
Flavon models in the literature assume constraints on the components of the vacuum expectation values (vevs) of flavons, and typically, these constraints are not fully determined by the residual symmetry group of the set of vevs. This poses a problem because the general potential of the flavons cannot have a minimum that leads to such constraints unless additional mechanisms involving supersymmetry, extra dimensions etc., are invoked. In this paper, we show that the framework of the auxiliary group naturally results in vevs satisfying the required constraints, and using it, we construct a type-1 seesaw model with two right-handed neutrinos, which predicts the ratio of the light neutrino masses m 2 / m 3 = ( 2 − 1 ) / ( 2 + 1 ) m_{2}/m_{3}=(\sqrt{2}-1)/(\sqrt{2}+1) and TM 1 \text{TM}_{1} mixing with sin ⁡ θ 13 = 1 3 ​ sin ⁡ π 12 \sin\theta_{13}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\sin\frac{\pi}{12} and sin ⁡ δ CP = − 1 \sin\delta_{\text{CP}}=-1 . Our framework posits auxiliary group transformations which act on the flavons but not on the fermions. We construct the general renormalizable potential without invoking additional mechanisms and show that it has a minimum that leads to the required constraints. 1 1 1 A video presentation of this paper is available here .



Almost every discrete flavour symmetry group in the literature is constructed as the direct product of a finite subgroup of
U(3)
and one or more Abelian groups. It was believed that there was no reason to go beyond
U(3)
to model flavour symmetries because there are only three families of fermions. However,
Babu and Gabriel 2010
;
Holthausen and Schmidt 2012
;
Holthausen et al. 2013a
went beyond this
U(3)
-subgroup paradigm by utilizing group extensions. By doing so, the authors succeeded in avoiding dangerous cross terms between the flavons in the charged-lepton and neutrino sectors. These cross terms, if present, would spoil the required constraints on the vevs by spoiling the residual symmetry groups obtained separately for the two sectors. Other papers in the literature assume mechanisms
Altarelli and Feruglio 2005
;
Altarelli and Feruglio 2006
;
He et al. 2006
;
Kadosh and Pallante 2010
;
Callen and Volkas 2012
involving supersymmetry, extra dimensions etc., to forbid the problematic cross terms.

In
Holthausen and Schmidt 2012
;
Holthausen et al. 2013a
, the flavons in the neutrino sector transform as a quartet representation of the flavour group
Q_{8}\rtimes A_{4}
while the fermions transform as multiplets isomorphic to the representions of
A_{4}
, i.e., the fermions form an unfaithful representation of the flavour group. Two quartet flavons are combined to obtain a multiplet isomorphic to the
A_{4}
triplet so that it can couple with the fermions in the neutrino sector. The authors showed that this construction generates accidental symmetries in the renormalizable potential and avoids dangerous cross terms. This approach is very appealing since it is minimal: it utilizes discrete symmetries and scalar fields only without requiring additional mechanims.

Refs.
Krishnan 2020
;
Krishnan 2022
put forward a framework that also went beyond the
U(3)
-subgroup paradigm. The author proposed that the
elementary
flavons transform as irreducible multiplets (irreps) of a group that is an extension of a
U(3)
-subgroup by an auxiliary group whereas the fermions transform as multiplets that are isomorphic to the irreps of the
U(3)
-subgroup. An
effective
irrep that can couple with the fermions was constructed by combining several elementary flavons. The author assigned vevs with specific residual symmetries to each elementary flavon, which led to constraints on the components of the vev of the effective irrep. These constraints did not originate from the residual symmetries of the vev under the
U(3)
-subgroup. Such constraints are similar to the ones proposed in the so-called indirect models
King 2015
and lead to highly predictive phenomenology.

Refs.
Babu and Gabriel 2010
;
Holthausen and Schmidt 2012
;
Holthausen et al. 2013a
and
Krishnan 2020
;
Krishnan 2022
used group extensions by auxiliary groups for different purposes. In the former, the purpose was to prevent dangerous cross terms among the various irreps such that they acquire vevs with specific residual symmetries. The later simply assigned residual symmetries to the vevs of the elementary irreps without addressing the problem of cross terms. In the later, the purpose was to obtain constraints in the vev of the effective irrep that are not attributable to its residual symmetries. In the current paper, the author kills both these birds using one stone, i.e., the author prevents the dangerous cross terms and obtains the aforementioned constraints using group extensions.

.1
2O
- the binary octahedral group

We construct the faithful
2
-dimensional irrep of the binary octahedral group,
2O
, using the generators

\mathbf{t}_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}\bar{\tau}&\bar{\tau}\\
-\tau&\tau\end{matrix}\right)\!,\,\quad\mathbf{r}_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}1&1\\
-1&1\end{matrix}\right)\!,\!

(1)

where
\tau=\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{2}}
,
\bar{\tau}=\frac{1-i}{\sqrt{2}}
are the complex eighth roots of unity. Let us call this irrep
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
. Let
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{R}
be the abstract elements of
2O
corresponding to
\mathbf{t}_{2}
and
\mathbf{r}_{2}
, respectively. In this paper, we use lowercase bold letters to denote square matrices, e.g.,
\mathbf{t}_{2}
,
\mathbf{r}_{2}
, and uppercase bold letters to denote abstract group elements, e.g.,
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{R}
.
2O
is a group with
48
elements. By taking the tensor product expansion of two
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
s of
2O
, we can understand its connection with the chiral octahedral group
S_{4}
,

\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}=\boldsymbol{3}\oplus\boldsymbol{1}.

(2)

The resulting
\boldsymbol{3}
is isomorphic to the triplet irrep of
S_{4}
. Its generators are given by

\mathbf{t}_{3}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\\
1&0&0\end{matrix}\right)\!,\,\,\mathbf{r}_{3}=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&-1&0\end{matrix}\right)\!.

(3)

\boldsymbol{3}
and
\boldsymbol{1}
in (
2
) correspond to the symmetric and the antisymmetric parts of the tensor product. Given
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
transforming as
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
, we construct
\boldsymbol{3}
as

(x,y)_{\boldsymbol{3}}=\!\big(x_{1}y_{1}+x_{2}y_{2},-i(x_{1}y_{2}+x_{2}y_{1}),i(x_{1}y_{1}-x_{2}y_{2})\big)^{\!{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\!\!\!.

(4)

S_{4}
is widely utilized for model building in the flavour physics literature
Pakvasa and Sugawara 1979
;
Brown et al. 1984
;
Lee and Mohapatra 1994
;
Mohapatra et al. 2004
;
Ma 2006
;
Hagedorn et al. 2006
;
Caravaglios and Morisi 2007
;
Zhang 2007
;
Koide 2007
;
Bazzocchi and Morisi 2009
;
Krishnan et al. 2013
. It has five irreps given by
\boldsymbol{1}
,
\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{2}
,
\boldsymbol{3}
and
\boldsymbol{3}^{\prime}
. These irreps of
S_{4}
form all the unfaithful irreps of
2O
. The singlet
\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}
remains invariant under
\mathbf{T}
and changes sign under
\mathbf{R}
. We may obtain the irrep
\boldsymbol{3}^{\prime}
as
\boldsymbol{3}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{3}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}
. The representation matrices of
\boldsymbol{2}
corresponding to
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{R}
are given by

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}=\left(\begin{matrix}\bar{\omega}&0\\
0&\omega\end{matrix}\right),\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{matrix}\right),

(5)

respectively, where
\omega=\frac{-1+i\sqrt{3}}{2}
,
\bar{\omega}=\frac{-1-i\sqrt{3}}{2}
are the complex cube roots of unity.

Besides
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
,
2O
has two more faithful irreps, which we denote by
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}
. We may obtain them as

\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}\otimes\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime},\quad\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}=\boldsymbol{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}.

(6)

By taking the Kronecker products of matrices representing
\boldsymbol{2}
(
5
) and
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
(
1
), we obtain the generators of
\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}
as

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{t}_{2}=\left(\begin{matrix}\bar{\omega}\mathbf{t}_{2}&0\\
0&\omega\mathbf{t}_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\!,\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes\mathbf{r}_{2}=\left(\begin{matrix}0&\mathbf{r}_{2}\\
\mathbf{r}_{2}&0\end{matrix}\right)\!,

(7)

representing
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{R}
, respectively. With
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
transforming as
\boldsymbol{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
, respectively, we obtain

(x,y)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}}=\left(x_{1}y_{1},x_{1}y_{2},x_{2}y_{1},x_{2}y_{2}\right)^{\scriptscriptstyle T}.

(8)

In our model, we also utilize the expansion,

\boldsymbol{3}^{\prime}\otimes\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}=\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}\oplus\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}.

(9)

With
\boldsymbol{3}^{\prime}\equiv x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}\equiv y=(y_{1},y_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
, the above expansion is given by

\displaystyle(x,y)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\!=\!(-x_{1}y_{2}+ix_{2}y_{1}+ix_{3}y_{2},x_{1}y_{1}-ix_{2}y_{2}+ix_{3}y_{1})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\!\!,

\displaystyle(x,y)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}}

\displaystyle\!=\!(-ix_{1}y_{2}\!-\!\omega x_{2}y_{1}\!-\!\bar{\omega}x_{3}y_{2},\,ix_{1}y_{1}\!+\!\omega x_{2}y_{2}\!-\!\bar{\omega}x_{3}y_{1},

\displaystyle\quad ix_{1}y_{2}\!+\!\bar{\omega}x_{2}y_{1}\!+\!\omega x_{3}y_{2},-ix_{1}y_{1}\!-\!\bar{\omega}x_{2}y_{2}\!+\!\omega x_{3}y_{1})^{{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\!\!.

(10)

We give more details about
2O
, such as its presentation and character table, in Appendix
A
. We also define the generalized CP
Ecker et al. 1987
;
Neufeld et al. 1988
;
Feruglio et al. 2013
;
Holthausen et al. 2013b
;
Ding et al. 2013
;
Chen et al. 2014
(gCP) for
2O
. We denote the generator of gCP by
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
. In Appendix
A
, we obtain the action of
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
on the irreducible multiplets of
2O
as

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{1}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{1}^{*}\!,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}}^{*}\!,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{2}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{2}^{*}\!,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{3}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{3}^{*}\!,\,\,\,\boldsymbol{3}^{\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{3}^{\prime*}\!,\!\!\!\\
&\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}\rightarrow i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{*},\quad\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}\rightarrow i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}{\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}}^{*},\quad\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}\rightarrow(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}^{*},\end{split}

(11)

where
∗
denotes complex conjugation, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}
are the first and second Pauli matrices, respectively.

.2
The model - with unbroken symmetries

We construct our model in the type-1 seesaw framework after incorporporating discrete flavour symmetries and gCP, which act on the Standard model leptons, a doublet of right-handed neutrinos and a set of flavons. We introduce seven flavon multiplets:
\chi\!=\!(\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\chi_{3})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
​,
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\!=\!({\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1},{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
​,
\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\!=\!({\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1},{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
​,
\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\!=\!({\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{1},{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
​,
\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\!=\!({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1},{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\!=\!({\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1},{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
​. Their components are complex numbers. In Section
.2.2
, we will show that the leading order flavon contribution in the construction of the Dirac mass term of the neutrinos is a
\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}
of
2O
obtained from the tensor product
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
. We denote this multiplet by
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
. Similarly, we will show that a
\boldsymbol{3}
of
2O
obtained from
\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\phi_{\mathrm{c}}
and denoted by
\phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
is the leading order contribution in the Majorana mass term. We call the multiplets
\xi
and
\phi
effective flavons as opposed to the earlier-mentioned seven flavons, which we call elementary.

\chi
and the effective flavons
\xi
and

transforming as the real or pseudo-real irreps of
2O
.
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
complexify these irreps such that we can impose gCP generated by
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
. Under
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
, the flavons transform as
\chi^{*}
​,
\phi^{*}
and
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\xi^{*}
. For the spinors,
\mathbf{CP}
is defined as
\mathbf{CP}\psi_{L}=-i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}\psi_{L}^{*}
and
\mathbf{CP}\psi_{R}=i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}\psi_{R}^{*}
. Since
N
is a
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
of
2O
, we have
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}N=i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}\mathbf{CP}N=i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N^{*}
where the first and the second
i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}
are acting on the
2O
and the spinor indices, respectively.
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
is equal to
\mathbf{CP}
for
L
,
\tau_{R}
,
\mu_{R}
and
e_{R}
. For the Higgs also,
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}=\mathbf{CP}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}H=H^{*}
.

shows how the leptons and the scalar fields transform under
2O
. We have the three families of left-handed weak isospin doublets of leptons forming a triplet under
2O
, denoted by
L=(L_{e},L_{\mu},L_{\tau})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
. The right-handed charged-lepton fields
e_{R},

\mu_{R}
and
\tau_{R}
are singlets. The right-handed neutrinos form a doublet under
2O
, denoted by
N=(N_{1},N_{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
. We introduce
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
, which generate the Abelian groups
Z_{3}
and
Z_{8}
, respectively, along with the generalized CP, under which the various fields transform. We call the flavour-gCP group generated by
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{R}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
and
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
, as defined by their actions on the fermions, TABLE
1
,
G_{r}
.

.2.1
The auxiliary generator

Let us introduce an auxiliary generator
\mathbf{X}
under which the elementary flavons
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{b}}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{c}}
transform while the fermions, the elementary flavon
\chi
and the effective flavons
\xi
and
\phi
remain invariant. Its introduction is a group extension of
G_{r}
. To explain how
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{b}}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{c}}
transform under
\mathbf{X}
and to achieve our group extension, we place the components of these flavons as well as the related effective flavons,
\xi
and
\phi
, inside matrices as given below,

\displaystyle\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\!\frac{1}{2}\!\left(\begin{matrix}\xi_{\mathrm{a}}&0&\xi_{\mathrm{a}}^{*}&0\\
0&\xi_{\mathrm{a}}&0&\xi_{\mathrm{a}}^{*}\end{matrix}\right)\!,

\displaystyle\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\!\left(\begin{matrix}{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}&0\\
0&{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}\\
0&{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{*}_{2}\\
{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{*}_{1}&0\end{matrix}\right)\!,

\displaystyle\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\!\left(\begin{matrix}{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}&{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}\\
{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}_{2}&-{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\!,

\displaystyle\llbracket\xi\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\!\left(\begin{matrix}\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}\\
\xi_{3}&\xi_{4}\end{matrix}\right)\!,\qquad\qquad

(12)

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\!\left(\begin{matrix}{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{1}&{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}^{*}_{2}\\
{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{2}&-{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}^{*}_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\!\!,\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle=\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\!\left(\begin{matrix}{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}&{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{*}_{2}\\
{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}&{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{*}_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\!,

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\!\left(\begin{matrix}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}&{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}\\
{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}_{2}&-{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\!\!,\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\llbracket\phi\rrbracket\!

\displaystyle=\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\!\left(\begin{matrix}\phi_{1}-i\phi_{3}&i\phi_{2}\\
i\phi_{2}&\phi_{1}+i\phi_{3}\end{matrix}\right)\!.

(13)

We call this kind of placement the
placeholder notation
2
2

2

We use the factors
\frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
in (
12
) and (
13
) for convenience so that the norms of the various multiplets are equal to the Frobenius norms of the corresponding matrices, i.e.,
|\xi_{\mathrm{a}}|^{2}=\text{tr}(\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket^{\dagger}\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket)
,
|\xi_{\mathrm{b}}|^{2}=\text{tr}(\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket^{\dagger}\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket)
, etc.
. Next, we make the following assignment,

\llbracket\xi\rrbracket=\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\,,\quad\llbracket\phi\rrbracket=(\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)_{\text{sym}}\,,

(14)

where
()_{\text{sym}}
denotes the symmetric part, i.e.,
(M)_{\text{sym}}=(M+M^{\scriptscriptstyle T})/2
. Given this assignment, we can obtain the components of
\xi
and
\phi
in terms of the components of the respective elementary flavons.

We define the actions of the generators
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{R}
on the elementary flavons
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{b}}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{c}}
by defining their actions on the the products
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket
in the following way,

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}:\,\,\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket({\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})^{\dagger}\,\,\,({\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\,\,\,\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\\
&\mathbf{t}_{2}\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\,\,\,\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\,\,\,\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\end{split}\,,

(15)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}:\,\,\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x})^{\dagger}\,\,\,(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x})\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\,\,\,\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\mathbf{r}_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\\
&\mathbf{r}_{2}\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\,\,\,\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\,\,\,\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\mathbf{r}_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\end{split}\,,

(16)

where
{\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2}
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix. As an example, let us evaluate the action of
\mathbf{R}
on
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
. The first line of (
16
) shows that
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x})^{\dagger}
under
\mathbf{R}
. This action keeps the positions of the zeros in
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket
(
12
) unchanged while exchanging
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
and
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}^{*}
. Therefore, (
16
) keeps the form of the matrix
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket
unchanged and results in
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rightarrow\xi_{\mathrm{a}}^{*}
, i.e., the action of
\mathbf{R}
on
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
is complex conjugation. We can verify that the placeholder notations of the elementary flavons
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket
,
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket
, etc., remain form-invariant under the group actions (
15
) and (
16
). We evaluate the corresponding actions on
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
etc., and list them in TABLE
2
.

We can also see that the matrices acting between the flavons, e.g.,
({\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})^{\dagger}
and
({\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})
between
\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket
in the first line of (
15
), are inverses of each other. Since these
internal
multiplications cancel each other, the overall group actions on the products
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket
are
external
, i.e., multiplying on the left and the right sides of these products. For
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket
, the left and the right multiplications are by
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}
and
\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
, respectively, in (
15
), and by
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}
and
\mathbf{r}_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
, respectively, in (
16
). These are the representation matrices corresponding to
\boldsymbol{2}
(
5
) and
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
(
1
), respectively. For
\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket
, on the other hand, the actions on both sides correspond to
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
(
1
). Given the tensor products (
6
) and (
2
), we can conclude that
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket
and the symmetric part of
\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket
transform as
\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}
and
\boldsymbol{3}
of
2O
, respectively, i.e.,
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\equiv(\xi_{\mathrm{a}},\xi_{\mathrm{b}},\xi_{\mathrm{c}})_{\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}}
and

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}:\begin{split}&\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes{\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2})^{\dagger}\,\,\,(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes{\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2})\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}^{\dagger}\,\,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket i\\

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}:\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket^{*}({\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2}\!\otimes\!\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x})^{\!\dagger}\,({\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2}\!\otimes\!\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x})\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket^{*}(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\!\dagger}\,(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket^{*}(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\!{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\!\!\\
&i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket^{*}\,\,\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket^{*}\,\,\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket^{*}(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\!{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\!\!\!\end{split}.

(18)

(

) and (

) lead to the transformations
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket i
and
\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket i
under
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
and
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}(\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)^{*}(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and
\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\rightarrow i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}(\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)^{*}(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\!{\scriptscriptstyle T}}
under
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
. These are in agreement with the transformation rules for the effective flavons given in TABLE
1
, i.e.,
\xi\rightarrow i\xi
and
\phi\rightarrow i\phi
under
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
and
\xi\rightarrow(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\xi^{*}
and
\phi\rightarrow\phi^{*}
under
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
.

Besides the generators (
15
)-(
18
), we introduce the auxiliary generator
\mathbf{X}
, whose action is defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\!:\!\begin{split}&\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes{\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2})^{\dagger}\,\,(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes{\scriptstyle{\mathbf{I}}}_{2})\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\dagger}\,\,(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\!\!\!\\
&\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\!\dagger}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\!\dagger}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket\!\!\!\!\end{split}\,\,\,\,\,\,,

(19)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\text{where}\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,\,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}=\text{diag}(\bar{\tau},\tau).

(20)

The action of
\mathbf{X}
on the products
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket
is only internal, which cancels. Therefore,
\mathbf{X}
acts trivially on the products and thus act trivially on
\xi
and
\phi
. We can verify that the group actions as defined in (
17
), (
18
) and (
19
) keep the placeholder notations of the elementary flavons
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket
,
\llbracket\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket
, etc., form-invariant. We evaluate the respective group actions on
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
, etc., and list them in TABLE
2
.

We call the flavour-gCP group generated by
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{R}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
,
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
and
\mathbf{X}
, as defined by their actions on all the fermions and the flavons, TABLE
1
,
2
,
G_{\!f}
. We verify that every multiplet in TABLE
2
is irreducible by studying the corresponding quotient group, i.e., the group generated by
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{R}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
,
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
and
\mathbf{X}
, as defined by their actions on the given multiplet. We perform this verification using the computational discrete algebra system GAP
The GAP Group 2022
without providing the details in the paper. The elements of
G_{\!f}
that act trivially on the fermions constitute a normal subgroup of
G_{\!f}
. We call it the auxiliary group
G_{x}
. The group
G_{r}
, which we defined at the beginning of Section
.2
, is the quotient of
G_{\!f}
by
G_{x}
, i.e., using our technique that invoves the placeholder notation, we extended
G_{r}
by
G_{x}
to obtain
G_{\!f}
3
3

3

We have
G_{\!f}=G_{x}\cdot G_{r}
, which denotes the extension of
G_{r}
by
G_{x}
. The group extension can be split or non-split. Split extension is a special case that corresponds to the semidirect product
G_{x}\rtimes G_{r}
. The direct product
G_{x}\times G_{r}
is a special case of
G_{x}\rtimes G_{r}
.
. In this paper, we study the action of the various generators on the irreps individually and then analyze the cross terms among the irreps. This approach does not require the characterization of the groups
G_{r}
,
G_{\!f}
and
G_{x}
as a whole.

.2.2
Construction of the Lagrangian

In this section, we determine the leading-order flavon contributions to the various mass terms in the Lagrangian. The fermion fields
\tau_{R}
and
\mu_{R}
transform as
\bar{\omega}
and
\omega
, respectively, under
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
. The only elementary flavon that transforms nontrivially under
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
is
\chi
. Therefore,
\chi
and
\chi^{*}
are the only leading order multiplets that can couple with
\tau_{R}
and
\mu_{R}
, respectively. The corresponding mass terms are
L^{\dagger}\chi\tau_{R}H+h.c.
and
L^{\dagger}\chi^{*}\mu_{R}H+h.c.
. Under
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
,
e_{R}
is invariant, hence we need a multiplet that is invariant under
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
and transforms as
\boldsymbol{3}^{\prime}
to couple with
L^{\dagger}
and
e_{R}
. Since such an elementary flavon does not exist in our model, we search for second-order flavon contributions. To aid us in finding the suitable pairs of flavons, we define the following group elements:
\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{\mathrm{a}}}=(\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X})^{8}
,
\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}=\mathbf{X}^{2}(\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}\mathbf{C}_{\sigma})^{2}
,
\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}\!=\!\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}^{2}\mathbf{X}^{2}
,
\mathbf{E}_{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}=\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}^{2}(\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}\mathbf{C}_{\sigma})^{2}
,
\mathbf{E}_{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}\!=\!\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}^{2}\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}^{2}\mathbf{X}^{2}(\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}\mathbf{X})^{12}
and
\mathbf{E}_{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}=\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}^{2}\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}^{2}\mathbf{X}^{2}
. How they act on the various multiplets in our model is shown in TABLE
3
.

.

L^{\dagger}e_{R}
is invariant under
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
as well as under
\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{\mathrm{a}}},..,\mathbf{E}_{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}
. As a consequence and by examining TABLE
3
, we can rule out every pair made with two different flavons, i.e., the only suitable pairs are the ones that involve the same flavon. For further analysis, we evaluate the tensor product of each flavon with itself,

\displaystyle\chi\otimes\chi

\displaystyle=|\chi|^{2}\oplus\chi^{2\alpha}\!\oplus\!\chi^{2\beta}\!\oplus\!\chi^{2\gamma}\!\oplus\!\chi^{2\delta}\!\oplus\!\chi^{2\epsilon}\!\oplus\!\chi^{2\zeta}\!,\!

(21)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{a}}

\displaystyle=|\xi_{\mathrm{a}}|^{2}\oplus{\xi_{\mathrm{a}}}^{\!\!\!2},

(22)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{b}}

\displaystyle=|\xi_{\mathrm{b}}|^{2}\oplus{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{\!\!\!2\alpha}\oplus{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{\!\!\!2\beta}\oplus{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{\!\!\!2\gamma}\oplus{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{\!\!\!2\delta},

(23)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{c}}

\displaystyle=|\xi_{\mathrm{c}}|^{2}\oplus{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}^{\!\!2\alpha}\oplus{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}^{\!\!2\beta}\oplus{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}^{\!\!2\gamma},

(24)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\phi_{\mathrm{a}}

\displaystyle=|\phi_{\mathrm{a}}|^{2}\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}^{\!\!\!2\alpha}\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}^{\!\!\!2\beta},

(25)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\!

\displaystyle=|\phi_{\mathrm{b}}|^{2}\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{\!\!\!2\alpha}\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{\!\!\!2\beta}\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{\!\!\!2\gamma}\!\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{\!\!\!2\delta},

(26)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\!\otimes\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\!

\displaystyle=|\phi_{\mathrm{c}}|^{2}\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}^{\!\!\!2\alpha}\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}^{\!\!\!2\beta},

(27)

where the expressions of the multiplets in the RHS are given in TABLE
5
. Throughout this paper, when a complex flavon multiplet appears in the LHS of a tensor product, it denotes the corresponding real irrep of twice the dimension, e.g., in (
21
),
\chi\otimes\chi
denotes
(\chi,\chi^{*})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\otimes(\chi,\chi^{*})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
. This is a consequence of using complex conjugation to represent group transformations. See Appendix
A
for a detailed discussion. In (
21
),
\chi\otimes\chi
produces
6\times 6=36
components, of which
21
form the symmetric part. In the RHS of (
21
)–(
27
), we have omitted the antisymmetric parts since they vanish. How the multiplets in the RHS transform is given in TABLE
5
. By examining it, we can see that the only multiplet that can couple with
L^{\dagger}e_{R}
is
\chi^{2\beta}
. Thus, we obtain the leading order mass term that involves
e_{R}
as
L^{\dagger}\chi^{2\beta}e_{R}H+h.c.
.

To construct the Dirac mass term, we consider the tensor product expansion
L^{\dagger}\!\otimes\!N=(L^{\dagger}\!,N)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}}\oplus(L^{\dagger}\!,N)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}}
, vis-a-vis (
9
). The expressions of
(L^{\dagger}\!,N)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}}
and
(L^{\dagger}\!,N)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}}
can be evaluated using (
10
) with the substitution
x=L^{\dagger}
and
y=N
. The product
L^{\dagger}\otimes N
changes sign under
\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}
and
\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}
and remains invariant under the other group elements listed in TABLE
3
. No elementary flavon has this transformation propertly. At the second order,
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
is the only combination that transforms in this way. However, under
\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{\mathrm{a}}}
,
L^{\dagger}\otimes N
and
\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
remain invariant while
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
and
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
transform nontrivially. Therefore, the only way to obtain a multiplet that can couple with
L^{\dagger}\otimes N
is to consider the third order and utilize the product
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
. We analyze the tensor product expansion of
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
in Appendix
B
. None of the resulting multiplets can couple with
(L^{\dagger}\!,N)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}}
. On the other hand, we find that only one multiplet exists that can couple with
(L^{\dagger}\!,N)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}}
, which is nothing but the effective flavon
\xi
defined in (
14
). Thus, we obtain the invariant term in the neutrino Dirac sector,
(L^{\dagger}\!,N)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\xi^{*}i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}H^{*}+h.c.
.

The transformation rules for the multiplets in the RHS of (

The Majorana mass term consists of the symmetric part of the product
i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N\!\otimes\!N
, where
i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}
is acting on the spinor index. According to (
2
), the symmetric part transforms as
\boldsymbol{3}
of
2O
, and we denote it by
(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N,N)_{\boldsymbol{3}}
. We can evaluate
(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N,N)_{\boldsymbol{3}}
using (
4
) with the substitution
x=i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N
and
y=N
. The product
i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N\!\otimes\!N
changes sign under
\mathbf{E}_{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}
,
\mathbf{E}_{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}
and
\mathbf{E}_{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}
, while remaining invariant under the other group elements in TABLE
3
. We can show that
\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\phi_{\mathrm{c}}
is the only leading-order combination of flavons that transforms in this way. We analyze this tensor product in Appendix
B
. Among the multiplets obtained, the only one that can couple with
(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N,N)_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is the effective flavon
\phi
defined in (
14
). This results in the invariant term in the Majorana sector,
(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N,N)_{\boldsymbol{3}}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\phi^{*}+h.c.
.

We summarize by writing down the Lagrangian,

\displaystyle\begin{split}-{\mathcal{L}}=&y_{\tau}L^{\dagger}\frac{\chi}{\Lambda}\tau_{R}H+{y_{\mu}L^{\dagger}\frac{\chi^{*}}{\Lambda}\mu_{R}H+y_{e}L^{\dagger}\frac{\chi^{2\beta}}{\Lambda^{2}}e_{R}H}+\\
&y_{\xi}(L^{\dagger}\!,N)_{\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\frac{\xi^{*}}{\Lambda^{3}}i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}H^{*}+\frac{y_{\phi}}{2}(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N,N)_{\boldsymbol{3}}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\,\frac{\phi^{*}}{\Lambda^{2}}\,+h.c.,\end{split}

(28)

where
\Lambda
is the cut-off scale. Invariance under gCP ensures that the Yukawa couplings
y_{\tau}
,
y_{\mu}
,
y_{e}
,
y_{\xi}
and
y_{\phi}
are real. We assume that all these couplings are of the order of one. The charged-lepton mass term, the neutrino Dirac mass term and the Majorana mass term in (
28
) can be written in matrix form as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}L_{e}\\
L_{\mu}\\
L_{\tau}\end{matrix}\right)^{\!\!\!\dagger}\!\!\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{y_{e}}{\Lambda}(\chi^{*}_{2}\chi_{3}+\chi^{*}_{3}\chi_{2})&y_{\mu}\chi^{*}_{1}&y_{\tau}\chi_{1}\\
\frac{y_{e}}{\Lambda}(\chi^{*}_{3}\chi_{1}+\chi^{*}_{1}\chi_{3})&y_{\mu}\chi^{*}_{2}&y_{\tau}\chi_{2}\\
\frac{y_{e}}{\Lambda}(\chi^{*}_{1}\chi_{2}+\chi^{*}_{2}\chi_{1})&y_{\mu}\chi^{*}_{3}&y_{\tau}\chi_{3}\end{matrix}\right)\!\frac{H}{\Lambda}\!\!\left(\begin{matrix}e_{R}\\
\mu_{R}\\
\tau_{R}\end{matrix}\right)\!,

(29)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}L_{e}\\
L_{\mu}\\
L_{\tau}\end{matrix}\right)^{\!\!\!\dagger}\!\!\left(\begin{matrix}-i\xi^{*}_{4}+i\xi^{*}_{2}&i\xi^{*}_{3}-i\xi^{*}_{1}\\
\bar{\omega}\xi^{*}_{3}-\omega\xi^{*}_{1}&-\bar{\omega}\xi^{*}_{4}+\omega\xi^{*}_{2}\\
\omega\xi^{*}_{4}-\bar{\omega}\xi^{*}_{2}&\omega\xi^{*}_{3}-\bar{\omega}\xi^{*}_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\!\!\frac{y_{\xi}i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}H^{*}}{\Lambda^{3}}\!\!\left(\begin{matrix}N_{1}\\
N_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\!,

(30)

\displaystyle\text{and}\,\,\,\left(\begin{matrix}i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N_{1}\\
i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y}N_{2}\end{matrix}\right)^{\!\!{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\!\left(\begin{matrix}\phi^{*}_{1}+i\phi^{*}_{3}&-i\phi^{*}_{2}\\
-i\phi^{*}_{2}&\phi^{*}_{1}-i\phi^{*}_{3}\end{matrix}\right)\frac{y_{\phi}}{2\Lambda^{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}N_{1}\\
N_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\!,

(31)

respectively. For the explicit expressions of
\xi
and
\phi
in terms of the components of the elementary flavons, please see (
67
) and (
78
), respectively, in Appendix
B
.

.3
Spontaneous symmetry breaking

The Higgs field acquires the vev
\langle H\rangle=(0,v)^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
, leading to the spontaneous breaking of electroweak symmetry. The flavour and gCP symmetries are broken at a higher energy scale when the elementary flavons acquire their vevs,

\displaystyle\langle\chi\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{\!\chi}}{\sqrt{3}}(-1,\omega,\bar{\omega})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\!\!,

\displaystyle\langle\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rangle\!

\displaystyle=\!r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{a}}}\bar{\omega},\qquad\quad\,\langle\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rangle=\frac{r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}}{\sqrt{2}}(\omega,\bar{\omega})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\!\!,\,\,\,\,\,\langle\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rangle\!=\!\frac{r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}}{\sqrt{2}}\tau(\bar{\varsigma},\varsigma)^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\!\!,\,

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rangle\!

\displaystyle=\!\frac{r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}}{\sqrt{2}}(1,\tau)^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\!\!,\quad\langle\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rangle\!=\!\frac{r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}}{\sqrt{2}}(\omega,1)^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\!\!,\quad\langle\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rangle\!=\!\frac{r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}}{\sqrt{2}}(\bar{\tau},1)^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\!\!\!,

(32)

where
\varsigma=e^{\frac{\pi}{8}}
and
\bar{\varsigma}=e^{-\frac{\pi}{8}}
, and
r_{\!\chi}
,
r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{a}}}
, etc., are the norms of the vevs. We will obtain these vevs as the minimum of the general renormalizable flavon potential by following the procedure developed by the author in a companion paper
Krishnan 2023
. First, we consider the flavons individually. We use arguments based on symmetries to show that the potential of each flavon is guaranteed to have a stationary point that corresponds to its vev (
.3
). In the second step, we show that the renormalizable cross terms among the flavons do not spoil these stationary points. The final step is to introduce driving flavons
4
4

4

If a flavon does not appear in the fermion mass terms at the leading order, we call it a driving flavon. This definition differs from that of the driving fields with
R
-charge equal to two proposed in supersymmetric flavour models, e.g.,
Altarelli and Feruglio 2005
.
to break accidental continuous symmetries that may be present in the renormalizable potentials of the individual flavons.

Ref.
Krishnan 2023
used theorems by Michel
Michel 1971
;
Michel and Zhilinskii 2001
, Golubitsky and Stewart
Golubitsky et al. 1988
to prove the existence of specific stationary points of the potential of an irrep without the need to explicitly extremize the potential. An alignment of an irrep is guaranteed to be a stationary point of the potential of the irrep if its stabilizer (residual symmetry group) under the symmetry group of the potential fully determines it up to its norm. The symmetry group of the potential of each flavon is the corresponding quotient group of
G_{\!f}
. We obtain the stabilizers of the vevs (
.3
) under these quotient groups. We list them along with their generators in TABLE
6
. We can see that these generators fully determine the vevs up to their norms. Hence, the vevs (
.3
) are guaranteed to be the stationary points of the corresponding potentials.

Our second step involves analyzing the cross terms among the flavons. TABLE
3
shows that
\chi
transforms as
\chi\rightarrow\omega\chi
under
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
. Since every other flavon is invariant under
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
, the contribution of
\chi
to cross terms cannot be linear. Rather,
\chi
may contribute quadratically from
\chi\otimes\chi
or cubically from
\chi\otimes\chi\otimes\chi
. Every flavon except
\chi
transforms nontrivially under
\mathbf{X}
. This rules out cubic contributions from
\chi\otimes\chi\otimes\chi
to cross terms. Under
\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}
,
\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}
,
\mathbf{E}_{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}
,
\mathbf{E}_{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}
and
\mathbf{E}_{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}
, the flavons
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{b}}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{c}}
, respectively, change sign, while every other flavon remains invariant. Therefore, these flavons cannot contribute linearly or cubically to cross terms. The flavon
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
cannot contribute cubically since we have ruled out a linear contribution from every other flavon. Under
\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{\mathrm{a}}}
, only
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
and
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
transform nontrivially. Therefore,
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
may appear linearly only in the combination
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
. None of the multiplets obtained from
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
, TABLE
5
, transforms in the same way as
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
, which rules out the combination
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
. Hence, we conclude that no flavon can appear linearly or cubically in cross terms.

Table 6:
Stabilizers of the vevs of the elementary flavons. They are isomorphic to
S_{3}
,
Z_{2}
and the Klein four group
K_{4}
.

TABLE
5
lists the multiplets obtained from the quadratic products of the flavons. No two rows of the table match, implying that no cross term can be constructed among these flavons, with the exception of the cross terms in the form of products of norms such as
|\chi|^{2}|\xi_{\mathrm{a}}|^{2}
. Ref.
Krishnan 2023
derived the
compatibility condition
which determines if the symmetries of a stationary point are preserved under the inclusion of a new term in the potential. The products of norms were shown to satisfy this condition. Therefore, the renormalizable cross terms that we obtained do not spoil the vevs (
.3
), i.e., they do not destroy the constraints among the components of these vevs.

We defer the final step to Appendix
D
. We find that the renormalizable potentials of several flavons contain accidental continuous symmetries. To break them, we introduce driving flavons. We need to ensure that they do not produce dangerous cross terms. To this end, the driving flavons are accompanied by group extensions implemented using auxiliary generators beyond
\mathbf{X}
. We construct the general renormalizable potential of all the flavons, including the driving flavons, and show that it has a minimum that corresponds to the vevs (
.3
).

.4
Phenomenology

Substituting
\langle\chi\rangle
(
.3
) in (
29
), we obtain the charged-lepton mass matrix,

M_{l}=\left(\begin{matrix}\,-y_{e}\frac{r_{\!\chi}}{\,\sqrt{3}\Lambda}&-y_{\mu}&-y_{\tau}\\
y_{e}\frac{r_{\!\chi}}{\sqrt{3}\Lambda}&\bar{\omega}y_{\mu}&\omega y_{\tau}\\
y_{e}\frac{r_{\!\chi}}{\sqrt{3}\Lambda}&\omega y_{\mu}&\bar{\omega}y_{\tau}\end{matrix}\right)v\frac{r_{\!\chi}}{\sqrt{3}\Lambda}.

(33)

We diagonalize
M_{l}
as

U_{l}M_{l}=\text{diag}(m_{e},m_{\mu},m_{\tau}),

(34)

\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\text{where}\qquad\quad\qquad U_{l}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\left(\begin{matrix}-1&1&1\\
-1&\omega&\bar{\omega}\\
-1&\bar{\omega}&\omega\end{matrix}\right),\qquad\qquad

(35)

\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\text{and}\quad m_{e}=y_{e}v\frac{r_{\!\chi}^{2}}{\sqrt{3}\Lambda^{2}}\,,\,\,m_{\mu}=y_{\mu}v\frac{r_{\!\chi}}{\Lambda}\,,\,\,m_{\tau}=y_{\tau}v\frac{r_{\!\chi}}{\Lambda}\,.\!\!\!\!

(36)

We define the ratio of the flavon vev and the cut-off scale as the factor
f
, i.e.,
f\approx\frac{r_{\!x}}{\Lambda}
where
r_{\!x}
denotes
r_{\!\chi}
,
r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{a}}}
, etc. According to (
36
), the masses of the muon and the tau are suppressed by
f
in relation to the Higgs vev, while the electron mass is suppressed by
f^{2}
. Knowing these masses, we can estimate that
f\approx 0.002
.

Substituting
\langle\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\rangle
,
\langle\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\rangle
,
\langle\xi_{\mathrm{c}}\rangle
and
\langle\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rangle
,
\langle\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rangle
,
\langle\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rangle
(
.3
) in (
67
) and (
78
), we obtain the vevs of the effective flavons,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\xi\rangle&=\frac{r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{a}}}r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}}{8\sqrt{2}}\big((-1+\sqrt{3})\bar{\varsigma}\omega,\,(-1-\sqrt{3})i\varsigma\omega,\\[-5.0pt]
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,\,(-1-\sqrt{3})\bar{\varsigma}\bar{\omega},\,(-1+\sqrt{3})i\varsigma\bar{\omega}\big)^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\!\!,\!\end{split}

(37)

\displaystyle\langle\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}}{8}(-1-i\sqrt{2},\,\sqrt{6},\,-1+i\sqrt{2})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}.

(38)

The constraints among the components of
\langle\xi\rangle
and
\langle\phi\rangle
are highly nontrivial. These constraints do not originate from the stabilizers of
\langle\xi\rangle
and
\langle\phi\rangle
under the group
G_{r}
. In this sense, these vevs are similar to those found in the indirect models in the literature. However, unlike in the previous works, we obtain these constraints using discrete symmetries alone without the help of mechanisms involving supersymmetry, extradimensions etc. The constraints on the vevs of the effective flavons (
37
), (
38
), originate indirectly from the stabilizers of the vevs of the elementary flavons (
.3
), TABLE
6
.

Substituting (
37
) and (
38
) in (
30
) and (
31
), we obtain the neutrino Dirac and the Majorana mass matrices,

\displaystyle M_{D}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{matrix}1&\tau\\
-\sqrt{2}&\sqrt{2}\tau\\
1&\tau\end{matrix}\right)\sqrt{3}\,\varsigma y_{\xi}v\frac{r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{a}}}r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}}{8\Lambda^{3}},

(39)

\displaystyle M_{M}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{matrix}\bar{\tau}(\sqrt{2}-1)&-\sqrt{3}\\
-\sqrt{3}&\tau(\sqrt{2}+1)\end{matrix}\right)iy_{\phi}\frac{r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}}{4\sqrt{2}\Lambda^{2}}.

(40)

Using (
39
) and (
40
), we obtain the seesaw mass matrix,

M_{ss}=-M_{D}M_{M}^{-1}M_{D}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}.

(41)

Since the mass terms in the neutrino Dirac sector and the Majorana sector are constructed with a single irrep each, i.e.,
\xi
and
\phi
, respectively, their vevs (
37
) and (
38
) fully constrain the neutrino mass matrices (
39
), (
40
) and (
41
) up to proportionality constants. Fully constrained mass matrices result in highly predictive models, e.g.,
Krishnan et al. 2018
.

We diagonalize
M_{ss}
in the following way:

U_{\nu}^{\dagger}M_{ss}U_{\nu}^{*}=\text{diag}(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}),

(42)

\text{where}\,\,\,\,U_{\nu}=\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
0&1&0\\
\frac{-1}{\sqrt{2}}&0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}1&0&0\\
0&i\cos\frac{\pi}{12}&-\sin\frac{\pi}{12}\\
0&i\sin\frac{\pi}{12}&\cos\frac{\pi}{12}\end{matrix}\right)\!\!,\!\!

(43)

m_{1}=0,\quad m_{2}=(\sqrt{2}-1)m_{\nu},\quad m_{3}=(\sqrt{2}+1)m_{\nu},

(44)

\text{with}\qquad m_{\nu}=\frac{3}{4}\frac{y_{\xi}^{2}}{y_{\phi}}\frac{v^{2}r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{a}}}^{2}r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{b}}}^{2}r_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{c}}}^{2}}{r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}r_{\!\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}\Lambda^{4}}\,.\qquad

(45)

Higher-order terms in the Lagrangian ensure a non-zero mass for the lightest neutrino. Even though we do not discuss such terms in this paper, we can infer that
m_{1}
is suppressed by at least a factor of
f\approx 0.002
in relation to the other neutrinos. Therefore, we use
m_{1}\approx 0
for our calculations in this section.

The PMNS neutrino mixing matrix is given by

U=U_{l}U_{\nu}.

(46)

Substituting (
35
)and (
43
) in the above equation and simplifying, we obtain

U=-\,\text{diag}(1,\omega,\bar{\omega})\,\,U_{\text{TM}_{1}}\!({\scriptstyle\frac{\pi}{12},\,\text{-}\frac{\pi}{2}})\,\,\text{diag}(1,-i,1),

(47)

where the expression of
U_{\text{TM}_{1}}\!({\scriptstyle\frac{\pi}{12},\,\text{-}\frac{\pi}{2}})
is provided in Appendix
C
. It denotes the
\text{TM}_{1}
mixing matrix
Xing and Zhou 2007
;
Albright and Rodejohann 2009
;
Albright et al. 2010
;
Antusch et al. 2012
;
de Medeiros Varzielas and Lavoura 2013
;
King 2013a
;
Luhn 2013
;
Li and Ding 2014
;
Zhao 2015
;
Gautam 2018
;
Shimizu et al. 2017
;
Rodejohann and Xu 2017
;
King 2013b
;
King 2016
;
King and Luhn 2016
;
Gautam 2018
;
Chakraborty et al. 2020
with its free parameters having values
\frac{\pi}{12}
and
-\frac{\pi}{2}
. The author recently showed
Krishnan 2022
that
U_{\text{TM}_{1}}\!({\scriptstyle\frac{\pi}{12},\,\text{-}\frac{\pi}{2}})
is consistent with the neutrino oscillation data. Please see Appendix
C
for a comparison of the predicted mixing angles and the CP phase with the data. The phase factors in the left side of
U_{\text{TM}_{1}}\!({\scriptstyle\frac{\pi}{12},\,\text{-}\frac{\pi}{2}})
in (
47
) are unobservable, while
-i
in the right side is a Majorana phase.

Global fit
de Salas et al. 2021
of the oscillation data gives the neutrino mass-squared differences,
\Delta m^{2}_{21}=75.0^{+2.2}_{-2.0}~\text{meV}^{2}
and
\Delta m^{2}_{31}=2550^{+20}_{-30}~\text{meV}^{2}
. From (
44
), we predict the ratio of the masses
m_{2}
and
m_{3}
,

\frac{m_{2}}{m_{3}}=\frac{\sqrt{2}-1}{\sqrt{2}+1}=\tan^{2}\frac{\pi}{8}.

(48)

This ratio is in excellent agreement with the data (within
1\sigma
errors) assuming
m_{1}\approx 0
. By fitting the data with (
44
), we obtain

m_{\nu}=20.92^{+0.08}_{-0.12}~\text{meV},

(49)

and also
m_{2}=8.66^{+0.13}_{-0.12}~\text{meV}
and
m_{3}=50.50^{+0.20}_{-0.30}~\text{meV}
.

Neutrinoless double-beta decay experiments
Dolinski et al. 2019
seek to determine the effective neutrino mass,

\langle m_{\beta\beta}\rangle=|U_{e1}^{2}m_{1}+U_{e2}^{2}m_{2}+U_{e3}^{2}m_{3}|,

(50)

where
U_{e1}
,
U_{e2}
and
U_{e3}
are the elements of the first row of the PMNS matrix. Substituting the values of these elements from (
79
) with
\theta=\frac{\pi}{12}
and using (
44
) and (
49
), we obtain

\langle m_{\beta\beta}\rangle\,\,=\,\,\frac{1}{3}\left(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}-1\right)\!m_{\nu}\,\,=\,\,1.57^{+0.01}_{-0.01}\text{ meV}.

(51)

This value is well below the upper bounds set by the recent
0\nu\beta\beta
experiments
Gando et al. 2016
;
Anton et al. 2019
;
Adams et al. 2022
, which are around
100~\text{meV}
. The sum of the neutrino masses,
\Sigma m_{i}
, is constrained by cosmological observations. The strongest upper bound provided by these observations is
90~\text{meV}

Di Valentino et al. 2021
;
Loureiro et al. 2019
. Since
m_{1}\approx 0
, we obtain

\Sigma m_{i}=\sqrt{\Delta m^{2}_{21}}+\sqrt{\Delta m^{2}_{31}}=59.16^{+0.32}_{-0.41}\text{ meV},

(52)

which is the lowest value consistent with the neutrino oscillation experiments.

Using (
45
), (
49
) and
f\approx 0.002
, we make the following order of magnitude estimates:
r_{\!x}=\mathcal{O}(10^{4}~\text{GeV})
,
\Lambda=\mathcal{O}(10^{7}~\text{GeV})
,
M_{D}=\mathcal{O}(1~\text{keV})
and
M_{M}=\mathcal{O}(100~\text{MeV})
. As
M_{D}
(
39
) contains three flavon insertions, it is heavily suppressed. The seesaw scale, i.e.,
M_{M}
, is correspondingly suppressed such that we obtain the correct masses for the light neutrinos. Low-scale seesaw models, such as ours, have interesting phenomenological consequences, e.g., they may result in heavy neutrinos detectable in the current and planned experiments
Abdullahi et al. 2023
. Since our model breaks the flavour-gCP symmetries at a relatively low scale compared to, say, the grand unification scale, we can make rather precise predictions without taking into account their running under the renormalization group.

.5
Summary

In this paper, we construct a type-1 seesaw model with two right-handed neutrinos and several flavons in the framework of the auxiliary group. According to this framework, the fermions transform under a finite group
G_{r}
while the elementary flavons transform under its group extension
G_{\!f}
. We have
G_{r}=G_{\!f}/G_{x}
, where
G_{x}
is the auxiliary group. In our model, the fermions transform as multiplets under the binary octahedral group generated by
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{R}
. We also assign the Abelian charges
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
and the generalized CP transformation
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
to the fermions. The actions of
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{R}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
and
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
on the fermions generate the flavour-gCP group
G_{r}
. We introduce the flavons
\chi
,
\xi
and
\phi
that transform as irreps under
G_{r}
and couple them with the fermions to form the charged-lepton, neutrino Dirac and Majorana mass terms, respectively.

We construct
\xi
and
\phi
as the effective flavons using
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
and
\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
, and
\phi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{b}}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{c}}
, respectively, which are the elementary flavons transforming as irreps of the extended flavour-gCP group
G_{\!f}
. To implement this group extension, we invent a technique that involves expressing the flavons in the
placeholder notation
. Using it, we define how
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{R}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
and
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
act on the elementary flavons
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{b}}
,
\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{b}}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{c}}
, while being consistent with how they act on the effective flavons
\xi
and
\phi
. Then, we introduce the auxiliary generator
\mathbf{X}
that acts nontrivially on these elementary flavons but trivially on
\xi
and
\phi
. We define
G_{\!f}
as the group generated by
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{R}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
,
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
and
\mathbf{X}
. In this way, our technique enables us to build
G_{\!f}
as a group extension of
G_{r}
. To simplify our analysis of the extended flavour group, we develop further techniques such as constructing specific group elements, TABLE
3
, under which the fermions and the flavons transform in a simple way, utilizing complex conjugation to represent group actions beyond gCP and combining a complex multiplet with its conjugate as a real irrep to evaluate tensor products. In Section
.2.2
and Appendix
B
, we verify that the leading-order contributions of the elementary flavons in the construction of the Dirac and Majorana mass terms are the effective irreps
\xi
and
\phi
obtained from
\xi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\xi_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\otimes\phi_{\mathrm{c}}
, respectively.

We obtain the vacuum expectation values (vevs) of the flavons with the help of the mathematical procedure developed in
Krishnan 2023
. To each elementary flavon, we assign a vev fully determined by its stabilizer, up to its norm. Such a vev is guaranteed to be a stationary point of the potential of the flavon. We analyze the renormalizable cross terms among the flavons and find that all of them are products of norms, which implies they satisfy the
compatibility condition
Krishnan 2023
. Therefore, the cross terms would not spoil our assigned vevs.

We explicitly construct the flavon potential in Appendix
D
. Renormalizable potentials of the various flavons contain accidental continuous symmetries. To break them, we introduce driving flavons after ensuring that they do not produce dangerous cross terms among themselves or with the other flavons appearing in the model. This is achieved by assigning specific auxiliary generators to the driving flavons, which results in further group extensions enlarging
G_{x}
and
G_{\!f}
. We show that the general renormalizable potential involving all the flavons has a minimum that corresponds to our assigned vevs. In Appendix
E
, we show that the driving flavons cannot provide any leading-order contribution to the mass terms.

We obtain the vevs of the effective flavons
\langle\xi\rangle
and
\langle\phi\rangle
using the vevs of the elementary flavons. The constraints in
\langle\xi\rangle
and
\langle\phi\rangle
are similar to those in indirect models and lead to highly predictive mass matrices. We obtain the mixing matrix to be in
\text{TM}_{1}
form with
\sin^{2}\theta_{13}=\frac{1}{3}\sin^{2}\frac{\pi}{12}
,
\sin^{2}\theta_{12}=1-\frac{2}{3\cos^{2}\theta_{13}}
,
\sin^{2}\theta_{23}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\sin\delta_{\text{CP}}=-1
. We estimate the lightest neutrino mass
m_{1}
to be sub-meV and predict the ratio of the other light neutrino masses as
\frac{m_{2}}{m_{3}}=\frac{\sqrt{2}-1}{\sqrt{2}+1}
. Using the experimental values of the neutrino mass-squared differences, we obtain
m_{2}=8.66^{+0.13}_{-0.12}~\text{meV}
,
m_{3}=50.50^{+0.20}_{-0.30}~\text{meV}
,
\langle m_{\beta\beta}\rangle=1.57^{+0.01}_{-0.01}\text{ meV}
and
\Sigma m_{i}=59.16^{+0.32}_{-0.41}\text{ meV}
. Since the Dirac mass matrix is suppressed by three flavon insertions, we have a relatively low-scale seesaw.

In this paper and in the companion paper
Krishnan 2023
, we set up the tools to construct flavon models in the framework of the auxiliary group, which does not assume mechanisms beyond the first principles of discrete symmetries and scalar fields. We construct such a model, predicting the neutrino mixing angles and the masses consistent with the current experimental data.

I am grateful to Paul Harrison, who persuaded me to seek ways to construct flavon models using discrete symmetries alone. Without his encouragement and support, I would not have been able to persevere in my attempts. I owe them everything for keeping a watchful eye on my physical and mental health these past few years, and I dedicate this work to them.

Appendix A
The group
2O

Abstractly, the binary octahedral group,
2O
, can be defined using the presentation,

\langle\mathbf{T},\mathbf{R}|(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{R})^{2}=\mathbf{T}^{3}=\mathbf{R}^{4},\mathbf{T}^{6}=1\rangle.

(53)

The unfaithful irrep
\boldsymbol{3}
of
2O
is isomorphic to the group
S_{4}
. Comparing (
53
) with the presentation of
S_{4}
,

\langle\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}|(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{R})^{2}=\mathbb{T}^{3}=\mathbb{R}^{4},\mathbb{T}^{3}=1\rangle,

(54)

we can see that the kernel of the irrep
\boldsymbol{3}
of
2O
is its
Z_{2}
subgroup generated by its element
\mathbf{T}^{3}
. In terms of the representation matrices of
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
(
1
) and
\boldsymbol{3}
(
3
), we have
\mathbf{t}_{2}^{3}=-1
and
\mathbf{t}_{3}^{3}=1
, as expected. The
Z_{2}
group generated by
\mathbf{T}^{3}
is a normal subgroup of
2O
. The quotient of
2O
by
Z_{2}
is nothing but
S_{4}
. Therefore,
2O
is a double cover of
S_{4}
5
5

5

2O
is the group extension of
S_{4}
by
Z_{2}
, i.e.,
2O=Z_{2}\cdot S_{4}
. This is not a split extension; therefore,
2O
cannot be expressed as the semidirect product, i.e.,
2O\neq Z_{2}\rtimes S_{4}
. In the recent literature
Novichkov et al. 2021
;
Liu et al. 2021
;
Ding et al. 2023
, a double cover of
S_{4}
, namely
S_{4}^{\prime}
, has been studied in the context of modular symmetries. We note that
2O
and
S_{4}^{\prime}
are different groups with quite different structures even though both are double covers of
S_{4}
.
. Using the computational discrete algebra system GAP
The GAP Group 2022
, we can obtain
2O
as SmallGroup(48,28).

We define the following group elements of
2O
,

\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{R}^{2},\quad\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{R}^{2}\mathbf{T}^{2}\mathbf{R},

(55)

and obtain their matrix representations under
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
and
\boldsymbol{3}
using (
1
) and (
3
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}(\boldsymbol{\underline{2}})=\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{matrix}\right),\qquad\quad\,\mathbf{U}(\boldsymbol{\underline{2}})=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{matrix}-1&1\\
1&1\end{matrix}\right),

(56)

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}(\boldsymbol{3})=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{matrix}\right),\quad\mathbf{U}(\boldsymbol{3})=-\left(\begin{matrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{matrix}\right).

(57)

The matrices
\mathbf{S}(\boldsymbol{3})
,
\mathbf{t}_{3}
(
3
) and
\mathbf{U}(\boldsymbol{3})
(or those isomorphic to them) are widely used to generate and study
S_{4}
in the flavour physics literature. We may obtain the abstract generator
\mathbf{R}
of
2O
from
\mathbf{S}
,
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{U}
as
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{T}^{5}\mathbf{U}
.

The character table of

.

\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
,
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}
are pseudo-real representations, also known as quaternionic representations. We require complex numbers to represent them, but their characters are real. The complex conjugation of pseudo-real representations are isomorphic to themselves. The representation matrices of
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
(
1
),
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}
(
6
) and
\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}
(
7
) are related to their complex conjugates through the similarity transformations,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}

\displaystyle=(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\,\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{*}\,(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\scriptscriptstyle T},

(58)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\,\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime*}\,(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\scriptscriptstyle T},

(59)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\,\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}^{*}\,(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\otimes i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}.

(60)

\boldsymbol{2}
is a real irrep even though, for convenience, we have used complex numbers (
5
) to construct it. For
\boldsymbol{2}
(
5
), the corresponding similarity transformation is

\boldsymbol{2}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\,\boldsymbol{2}^{*}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}^{\scriptscriptstyle T}.

(61)

After introducing flavons transforming as irreps under a non-Abelian finite group, flavour models may impose additional Abelian transformations, such as
Z_{3}
, on the irreps. This complexifies the real and pseudo-real irreps, i.e., changes them to complex irreps, and enlarges the flavour group. Models may also impose complex conjugation on the flavons as a symmetry transformation, which usually corresponds to the CP transformation. For the complex conjugation to be consistent
Holthausen et al. 2013b
with the flavour group, a similarity transformation must exist such that the combined action of the conjugation and the similarity transformation is an automorphism on the representation matrices of the flavour group. This combined action is called the generalized CP (gCP)
Ecker et al. 1987
;
Neufeld et al. 1988
;
Feruglio et al. 2013
;
Holthausen et al. 2013b
;
Ding et al. 2013
;
Chen et al. 2014
.

The irreps
\boldsymbol{1}
,
\boldsymbol{1}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{3}
,
\boldsymbol{3}^{\prime}
of
2O
are real, and we have used real matrices to construct them. Therefore, complex conjugation maps every element of these irreps to itself. For
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}
,
\boldsymbol{\underline{2}}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{\underline{4}}
and
\boldsymbol{2}
also, conjugation combined with the corresponding similarity transformation (
58
)-(
61
), maps every element to itself. Given these mappings, let us define the action of gCP on the irreducible multiplets as (
11
). This definition implies that gCP maps every representation matrix to itself, i.e., gCP produces the trivial automorphism. This is expected because the irreps of
2O
are real or pseudo-real. On the other hand, we can show that gCP (
11
) produces a nontrivial automorphism if we complexify the irreps using the aforementioned Abelian transformations. Therefore, our definition of gCP is consistent and nontrivial for the complexified irreps of
2O
.

There are two equivalent ways to approach the flavour and gCP symmetries: either construct the flavour group and study gCP separately as an automorphism or study them together by constructing the flavour-gCP group as the semidirect product
Holthausen et al. 2013b
of the flavour group (normal subgroup) and the gCP group (quotient). In this paper, we follow the second approach. We construct
2O
using the generators
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{R}
. In Section
.2
, we complexify the irreps by introducing the generators
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
, and then introduce gCP using the generator
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
. Using these five generators, we construct the flavour-gCP group
G_{r}
. Complex irreps in the flavour group become real irreps of twice the dimension in the flavour-gCP group. For example, in TABLE
1
, we introduce
\chi
as a
\boldsymbol{3}^{\prime}
of
2O
, which gets complexified by
\mathbf{Z}_{l}
. With the inclusion of gCP as a group transformation,
\chi
becomes
(\chi,\chi^{*})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
transforming as a six-dimensional real irrep.

We may use complex conjugation to represent group transformations other than gCP also. For example, in TABLE
2
, complex conjugation appears in relation to not only
\mathbf{C}_{\sigma}
but also
\mathbf{R}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}
and
\mathbf{X}
. Every flavon expressed as a complex multiplet in this paper undergoes complex conjugation as a part of one or more group generators. Hence, every one of them transforms as a real irrep of twice the dimension. We use complex multiplets to represent them because of the ease of construction and analysis. This usage is justified because all degrees of freedom in the real multiplet are present in the corresponding complex multiplet, and all group actions on the real multiplet are faithfully represented with the help of matrix multiplications and complex conjugation on the complex multiplet. However, evaluating tensor products using these complex multiplets results in difficulties, similar to those encountered when gCP is studied separately as an automorphism rather than studied together with the rest of the group
Holthausen et al. 2013b
. To avoid these difficulties, we use real irreps for evaluating the tensor products, e.g.,
\chi\otimes\chi
denotes
(\chi,{\chi}^{*})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\!\otimes(\chi,{\chi}^{*})^{\scriptscriptstyle T}
.

is invariant under it, hence the irrep obtained from

and
{\phi_{\mathrm{bc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!2\alpha}
are invariant while
{\phi_{\mathrm{bc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!2\beta}
transforms as
{\phi_{\mathrm{bc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!2\beta}\rightarrow\bar{\omega}{\phi_{\mathrm{bc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!2\beta}
under
(\mathbf{Z}_{\nu}\mathbf{X})^{4}
. Therefore, we rule out
{\phi_{\mathrm{bc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!2\beta}
from our construction. Using
{\phi_{\mathrm{bc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!2\alpha}
, we obtain

\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes{\phi_{\mathrm{bc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!2\alpha}={\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\alpha\,\,\,}\,\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\beta\,\,\,}\,\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\gamma\,\,\,}\,\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\delta\,\,\,}\,\,\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\epsilon\,\,\,}\,\,\oplus{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\zeta\,\,\,.}

(71)

where

{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\alpha\,\,\,}

\,=(\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)_{\text{sym}},

(72)

{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\beta\,\,\,}

\,=(\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)_{\text{asym}},

(73)

{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\gamma\,\,\,}

\,=(\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!z}\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)_{\text{sym}},

(74)

{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\delta\,\,\,}

\,=(\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!z}\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)_{\text{asym}},

(75)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{\small${\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\epsilon\,\,\,}$}&\text{\small$\,=\big((\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\!+\!i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)_{\text{sym}},$}\\
&\qquad\text{\small$(\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\!-\!i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)_{\text{sym}}\big)^{\!{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\!\!\!,$}\end{split}

(76)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{\small${\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\zeta\,\,\,}$}&\text{\small$\,=\big((\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\!+\!i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)_{\text{asym}},$}\\
&\qquad\text{\small$(\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{a}}\rrbracket(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!x}\!-\!i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\!y})\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{b}}\rrbracket\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{c}}\rrbracket)_{\text{asym}}\big)^{\!{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\!\!\!,$}\!\!\end{split}

(77)

with ‘sym’ and ‘asym’ denoting the symmetric and the antisymmetric parts of the matrices. How these irreps transform is given in TABLE
8
. Every irrep except
{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\alpha\,\,\,}
and
{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\beta\,\,\,}
transforms nontrivially under
\mathbf{X}
, hence they cannot couple in the Majorana mass term. Since the Majorana mass matrix is symmetric, we discard the antisymmetric contribution
{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\beta\,\,\,}
. Hence, we are left with only
{\phi_{\mathrm{abc}}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!3\alpha\,\,\,}
, which is equal to
\phi
(
14
). Its expression is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{\small$\phi\,\,$}&\text{\small$=\frac{1}{4}\big({\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{1}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}+{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}^{\!\!*})+{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{2}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}-{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}^{\!\!*})$}\\
&\text{\small$\hskip 18.49988pt\,\,+{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{2}^{\!\!*}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}+{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}^{\!\!*})-{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}-{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}),$}\\
&\text{\small$\hskip 18.49988pt-i{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{1}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}-{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}^{\!\!*})-i{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{2}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}+{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}^{\!\!*})$}\\
&\text{\small$\hskip 18.49988pt\,\,-i{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{2}^{\!\!*}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}-{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}^{\!\!*})+i{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}+{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}^{\!\!*}),$}\\
&\text{\small$\hskip 18.49988pt+i{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{1}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}+{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}^{\!\!*})-i{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{2}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}-{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}^{\!\!*})$}\\
&\text{\small$\hskip 18.49988pt\,\,+i{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{2}^{\!\!*}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}+{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}^{\!\!*})+i{\phi_{\mathrm{a}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}({\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{2}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{2}-{\phi_{\mathrm{b}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}{\phi_{\mathrm{c}}}_{1}^{\!\!*}\big)^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\!\!.$}\end{split}

(78)

Appendix C
\text{TM}_{1}
mixing and the oscillation data

\text{TM}_{1}
mixing
Xing and Zhou 2007
;
Albright and Rodejohann 2009
;
Albright et al. 2010
;

;
de Medeiros Varzielas and Lavoura 2013
;

;

;

;

;

;

;

;

;

;
Gautam 2018
;
